CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION 

The improvement in the position of small business which took form in mid-1954 -- after a decline in small business health over several years continued through the second half of 1955. 

This gain is reflected in various economic indicators. Throughout most of 1955 the trend of business failures was downward. New business incorporations hit a new peak. There was sharp improvement in small firms' earnings, both before and after taxes. For the four quarters ending with the third quarter 1955, earnings of small corporations after taxes increased to $627 million from $442 million in the preceding four quarters. 

Net sales of smaller manufacturing corporations continued their recovery. 

The various programs of the Small Business Administration designed to help small business registered notable gains. 

During the 6-months period July 1 through December 31, 1955, the Small Business Administration approved 625 business loans in the gross amount of $30, 332, 390 to assist small firms. This was 

20 per cent greater than business loan approvals in the previous 

6-months period.

The Agency's Financial Assistance Program is carried on in close co-operation with the banks of the Nation. As evidence of this, more than 66 per cent of the Agency's business loans have been in participation with banks.

The severe hurricanes and consequent floods in the late summer and fall of 1955 resulted in widespread damage to homes and businesses, particularly in the Northeastern States. The Small 

Business Administration immediately classified the localities as disaster areas eligible for assistance under the Agency's disaster loan authority. Through the co-operation of local banks and through the transfer of personnel from the Washington, regional and branch offices, the Small Business Administration was able to organize emergency offices in each of these areas within a few days after the 1

2 disasters occurred.

For the 6-months period from July 1 through 

December 31, 1955, the Agency approved 2, 056 disaster loans aggregating $29, 290, 391 to assist business and home owners. This compares with 813 disaster loans for $4, 733, 423 during the similar period of 1954.

A significant development in the program of financial assistance was the inauguration of the Limited Loan Participation Program. This is designed especially to assist small retailers, wholesale distributors and service establishments. Under this plan the Small Business Administration will participate with a bank in a loan to a small retailer, wholesaler, service establishment or other business up to a maximum of $15,000 or 75 per cent of the total amount of the loan, whichever is the lesser. The early experience under this program gives ground for belief that under this plan banks, with Small Business Administration co-operation, will be able to meet the financial needs of a considerably greater number of small business concerns than has heretofore been possible. 

There was marked progress in the Joint Determination Program, the program for reserving Government purchases for small business participation. In the 6-months period since July 1, more than 2, 100 purchases, amounting to approximately $192, 800, 000, were earmarked for competitive award to small firms. This compares with $173,000,000 in the comparable period of 1954. The number of procurements awarded to small firms increased even more sharply to 2,028, compared with 854 for the 6-months period ending December 1954 and 256 for the same period of 1953. The Department of Defense has been able to increase total awards to small concerns; on the basis of new procurement, total share of small business in the defense dollar during the 6-months period July 1 through December 31, 1955, increased to $1, 382, 759, 000 from $1,222, 000, 000 for the same period a year earlier.

There is a prospect for even greater participation by small business in Government purchasing as a result of three Department of Defense directives, one of which was issued in the last 6 months, calling for greater set-asides for small business concerns. 

The Small Business Administration co-operated closely with the Defense Department in the development of these directives. 

In the area of production and management assistance, much time has been given to assisting small businesses obtain raw materials

in scarce supply. These include steel, steel scrap, aluminum, copper, nickel, newsprint, lead, plate glass and other materials. 

Shortages, bottlenecks and price increases are characteristic of the boom economy which we are experiencing. 

The Small Business Administration will continue to expand its assistance to small business by broadening and improving its present programs.

CHAPTER II ECONOMIC POSITION OF SMALL BUSINESS 

During the second half of 1955, the national economy continued to expand. The gross national product, which was $384. 8 billion in the second quarter of the year, rose to $397. 3 billion by the last quarter. National income rose from $320. 7 billion in the second quarter to $330. 8 billion in the last quarter. Other economic indexes also hit new highs.

The continued expansion of the national economy was accompanied by further improvement in the status of small business. 

The improvement in the position of small business which took form in mid-1954 -- after a decline in small business health over several years -- continued throughout the second half of 1955. As will be demonstrated later, this gain was reflected in various indicators such as small firms' sales, their earnings before and after taxes, and their earnings on stockholders' equity. National defense outlays today are stabilized at a lower level than previously, but a greater amount of money is being spent for civilian goods, and small business is getting a greater share of these expenditures. 

There are, of course, various problems which plague the small business sector of the economy. Foremost among them is the squeeze on some raw materials resulting from the high level of economic activity. Small businesses complain of inability to obtain steel, steel scrap, aluminum, copper, nickel, newsprint, lead, plate glass and other materials. They complain also of higher prices, such as for steel scrap, which they are unable to absorb. They point out that the historical allotment system used by suppliers discriminates against small firms because they are unable to obtain greater allotments, and, if they are new users, sometimes are not able to get supplies at all. Shortages, bottlenecks and price increases which are characteristic of a boom economy belie the threat of recession despite the sags in some individual sectors which may occur, like that in the automotive field. 5

Other problems of small firms include continued lack of subcontracting opportunities because of cutbacks in military purchasing, competition from imports and lack of foreign trade opportunities, and inability to compete with larger firms for skilled professional help, such as engineers.

Many small firms complain also of tightening bank credit; on the other hand, the earlier expansion and large amount of outstanding bank credit permitted by the Federal Reserve Board has enabled many small firms to obtain financing through private sources. 

Small firms report that they continue to face a problem of finding equity capital financing. However, Securities and Exchange Commission statistics reveal that the improved security market has enabled a far larger number of small concerns to obtain equity financing than at any time since 1929.

The Business Population 

The number of businesses is gradually increasing 

4,225,000 as of June 30, 1955, compared with 4, 196, 700 a year earlier. Of these, 4, 000, 000 are small. A total of 355, 400 new businesses started during the year more than offset 327, 100 discontinuances. The total number of businesses in operation is about one-third larger than at the close of World War II. 

At the end of 1954, about half the Nation's business firms had been in operation at least 6. 75 years compared with 2. 75 years at the end of 1947. In 1953, gross national expenditures in 1947 dollars per firm were $73, 400 compared with $61, 400 in 1947. 

Beginning with 1947 through 1953, the percentage of firms surviving 1 year or more decreased from 68 to 62 per cent. 

The per cent of those surviving 2 years or more decreased from 50 per cent in 1947 to 45 in 1952. This decline was partly due to the abnormally large number of businesses started immediately after the war and partly due to the hard competition which followed as soon as the backlog of demand for consumer goods was satisfied. 

To,some degree the high proportion of older firms is a factor of strength in the business economy. Business operators today generally have had more experience and do a larger volume of business than during the first few years after the war. This emphasizes the need for better training and more counseling or a

wider experienced management staff for new entrepreneurs who must compete with firms which are well established. 

The age distribution of the business population as of 

December 31, 1954, was as follows:

Preliminary 1954 Census data for several States suggest that the number of retail food stores may have declined between 1948 and 1954. The number in other retail trades appears to have changed relatively little or increased. 

Increase in New Business Incorporations 

The increase in business incorporations is a significant index of the business boom. Last year there was a record number of business incorporations 139, 639 compared with 117, 164 in 1954. In all but one month of 1955 the number of business incorporations was higher than in the corresponding month of 1954, virtually paralleling the situation in 1954, when each month brought a greater number of incorporations than in the corresponding month of 1953 as shown in chart 1 on page 8. While the 1955 increase for the country as a whole was about one-fifth greater than the 1954 total of new incorporations, it was nearly one-third greater in the Southern and Western States. 

Statistics on business incorporations include not only establishment of new businesses but also conversion of existing businesses into corporations. While the increase in new business incorporations is due partly to changes in the tax law of benefit to firms which use the corporate form, there is no doubt that the high level of economic activity also is a significant factor. 

Decline in Number of Business Failures 

Throughout most of 1954, the trend of business failures was visibly downward and in 1955 business failures were slightly

below 1954. In 1955 the total number of business failures reported by Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., was 10,969 compared with 11, 086 in 

1954. The 1955 rate of failures was unchanged over 1954 -- 42 for each 10,000 enterprises listed in the Dun & Bradstreet Reference Book. Actually, failures in 1954 and 1955 have been low in relation both to the annual average number and rate of failures. For the period 1900-1955, the average number was 13,612 and the rate was 

70 per 10,000 business enterprises.

The monthly totals of business failures in 1955 compared with 1954 are shown in chart 2 on page 10. Also, the 1955 monthly average of 914 is compared with the average of 1, 134 for the period 1900-1955. 

The annual rates of business failures for the period 19001955 are shown in chart 3 on page 11. The average rate was 70. 

Against this background, it is obvious that the current failure rate of 42 is not high.

As might well have been expected during World War II and during the Korean hostilities, the rate of failures was lower than at present. It was lower also during the postwar years because of the unlimited demand for goods. 

A normal year for the purpose of considering business failures would be one in which the country was neither at war nor reconverting from war. On that basis, the rate of business failures during 1954 and 1955 is the lowest ever occurring since records have been kept in this country during a normal year. 

There were some shifts in the composition of failures in 1955 as compared with 1954. For example, failures of retailers of furniture and home furnishings and in the automotive group decreased sharply. On the other hand, there was some increase in the food and liquor, apparel and lumber and building materials retail trades. 

Improved Financial Position of Small Business 

The improvement in the financial position of small business which became evident in 1954 continued through 1955. As shown in the table on page 12, quarterly earnings of United States manufacturing corporations increased steadily. This upward trend was characteristic of both smaller and larger corporations. improvement in small firms' earnings both before and after taxes. 

For the four quarters ending with the third quarter 1955, small corporations' earnings before taxes increased to $1,245 million compared with $934 million for the four preceding quarters. 

Similarly, small corporations' earnings after taxes increased to $627 million from $442 million.

Net sales of smaller manufacturing corporations, those with assets under $1,000, 000, continued their recovery during 1955. 

During the second and third quarters of the year, net sales of these firms were $18, 123, 000, 000 compared with $16, 813, 000, 000 in the corresponding quarters of 1954. This trend of increasing sales was evident in mid-1954.

Higher Earnings Before Taxes 

The earnings before taxes situation of small corporations continues to show substantial improvement. During the second quarter of 1955, small corporations' earnings increased to $351, 000, 000 compared with $327, 000, 000 in the same quarter the year before. In the third quarter, the increase in small corporations' earnings was even greater, reaching $446, 000, 000, compared with $341,000,000 in the like period of 1954.

The level of earnings, as might be expected, is still far below the peaks reached by small corporations during the expanded production of the Korean period; on the other hand, the high level of earnings before taxes which large manufacturing corporations achieved during the Korean period has been maintained except for minor fluctuations. 

Increased Earnings After Taxes

Similarly, the earnings after taxes of small corporations continue to increase. In the second quarter of 1955, they were approximately the same as in the second quarter of 1954, but in the third quarter they reached a level of $252, 000, 000 compared with

$184, 000, 000 the year before.

Although small corporations' profits are still relatively much lower than those of large manufacturers compared with the base period years 1947-49, there is now definite evidence of an upward trend in earnings of the smaller firms. This is encouraging because the impact of taxes on the small manufacturer has been a factor in limiting the amount of internal financing by them. 

For the four quarters ending with the third quarter of 1955, the earnings after taxes index for smaller corporations reached 67 compared to 46 a year earlier; for the larger corporations, the index was up to 

127 compared to 101 as shown in chart 5 on page 15. 

Greater Earnings on Stockholders' Equity 

Earnings on stockholders' equity offer another comparison of earnings of large and small manufacturing corporations. Stockholders' equity is composed of the amounts of capital stock outstanding, earned surplus or undivided profits, surplus reserves and that portion of other reserves which is considered surplus. 

In 1947 earnings before taxes on stockholders' equity of the smaller corporations (those with assets under $1 million) were 27.8 per cent. The earnings of the larger corporations were 25. 3 per cent. During the recession of 1949, the annual rates of earnings declined. They rose sharply in 1950. But, by 1952, they had declined to 16.9 per cent for the smaller corporations and 22. 7 per cent for the larger ones.

The downward trend in earnings of the smaller corporations continued through 1953 into 1954. However, beginning in 

1954 and through 1955, there was an improvement for both smaller and larger corporations. For the four quarters ending with the third quarter 1955, the annual rate of earnings for the smaller corporations was 13.7 per cent and for the larger corporations 22.9 per cent, as shown in chart 6 on page 17. The changes in the annual rates of earnings after taxes was similar, as may be observed from the table on page 16.

Financing of Manufacturing Corporations 

Compared with the small manufacturers, the larger corporations are able to obtain long-term debt financing at an appreciably lower cost, to retain earnings, or to obtain funds economically through equity financing. Small manufacturers are forced to rely relatively more on their creditors and on short-term

For example, smaller corporations with total assets of $250,000 to $1,000,000 at the end of 1954 financed 18. 7 per cent of their total assets either through notes and accounts payable or through bank loans payable within 1 year. On the other hand, the largest corporations, those with $100 million or more of total assets, financed only 8.1 per cent. Long-term debt financing of the smaller corporations was 7.2 per cent of total assets. This type of financing of the largest corporations was 12.8 per cent. 

The percentage of cash, United States Government and other marketable securities held by smaller corporations was 14.0 per cent of total assets. In the case of the largest corporations, it was 16. 2 per cent.

Another serious disadvantage of smaller corporations is lack of adequate liquid assets to finance properly their receivables and inventories. That is, a lower proportion of total funds of the smaller corporations was available to finance fixed assets.

Outside funds obtained by small business single proprietor ships and partnerships as well as corporations are substantially below the amounts wanted. A recent study by the Department of 

Commerce shows that of the firms included in the survey 20. 1 per cent had not been able to obtain the funds they wanted. 

13.3 per cent obtained some funds, 6.8 per cent obtained none. 

As would be expected, the survey revealed that established firms did better than the newer firms. While While a portion of the small firms' unfilled demand for funds could not and perhaps should not be met by lenders or investors, a significant portion of the unmet demand was never brought to the attention of lenders and therefore was not included in the above percentages. This was because some firms felt there was "no use in trying" either on the basis of past experience or "common knowledge." Short-term financing dominated the funds obtained but funds which were not obtained were predominantly of an intermediate or long-term nature.

The median amount of funds obtained by established firms was $8, 600 and by newer firms $4,500. Amounts wanted but not obtained were the same for the newer and older concerns, averaging $10,000.

The Commerce Department study points up the unequal impact of Federal taxation on small businesses and their need for more equity capital. The need of small businesses for smaller sums over an intermediate period (1 to 5 years) was one of the main reasons why the Small Business Administration recently established its Limited Loan Participation Program as explained on page 38. 

Equity Capital for Small Business 

The year 1955 was a banner one for flotation of new securities in the securities markets. The following table shows the great increase in securities issues in 1955, as compared with 1954: 1954 1955 

Total Securities Offerings 

$9,516, 000, 000 $10, 454, 000, 000

Securities Issues Between 

$100,000 and $300, 000

269, 000, 000

During the latter part of 1955, the number of uranium issues dropped sharply; nonetheless, new issues continued to exceed $20 million a month. This indicates that new opportunities which have not existed since 1929 have been opened for common stock financing for small companies. This is due to the lessened attractiveness of well-established issues to many investors because of the high levels of the market and the opportunities for capital gains in smaller issues.

The Securities and Exchange Commission has been considering for some months adoption of a revision of the regulations to tighten up requirements for issues of promotional companies. This revision, aimed principally at uranium and other speculative-type issues, might also impair financing possibilities for new, small firms, since

some of them might be regarded as promotional companies. Up to this time, the proposed revision is still under consideration. 

There will be renewed attempts in the present session of Congress to extend the $300, 000 exemption to issues under $500,000. This would broaden the use of Regulation A by small companies.

The record year for securities offerings is particularly notable when considered in conjunction with the fact that internal financing is providing a continually greater source of capital for business. Smaller businesses have less retained earnings to plow back than the larger companies. For the first three quarters of 1955, the percentage of retained earnings of all corporations rose to 50 per cent compared to 41 per cent in the first three quarters of 1954, a striking increase in this method of raising equity capital. 

Development Credit Corporations 

Statewide institutions known as development credit corporations or business development corporations, first established in the New England States, have now spread to many other States. The fundamental purpose of these corporations is to pool loan funds advanced to them by financial institutions and to grant intermediate and long-term loans to small businesses which are considered good risks but which have been unable to obtain money from other sources. The Small Business Adminis tration is co-operating with both statewide and local development corporations and has made loans in participation with them. 

The latest report on the activities of the New England development credit corporations is for the 6 months, ending June 30, 1955. During that period the Development Corporation of Maine approved 10 loans for a total of $371,000; New Hampshire, 3 loans for $79, 000; Massachusetts, 14 loans for $2, 465, 000; Rhode Island, 1 loan for $60,000; Connecticut, 10 loans for $466,000. The total is 38 loans for $3,441,000. On a cumulative basis, the New 

England credit corporations have approved 163 loans for a total of $9,636, 499. The cumulative total of losses incurred by all of the 

New England development credit corporations is $8, 346. That amount is listed for the Maine corporation. 

The Small Business Administration wishes to promote the activities of and co-operate with local development corporations

which are interested in furthering the growth and expansion of industry in their communities. Such corporations will increase not only the well-being of their local communities but also the wellbeing of the Nation by giving industries the advantages of the optimum use of natural resources, freight rates, labor supply, proximity to markets and other economic advantages. At the same time, the 

Small Business Administration will not promote the relocations of industries in which the primary incentive is a local subsidy or in which relocation is either from an area of present labor surplus or in which the relocation will cause a labor surplus. 

In carrying out this objective the Small Business Adminis tration will consider applications for loans to development credit corporations where the corporation submits a firm plan satisfactory to this Agency establishing that the proceeds of the loan will assist an existing small business concern otherwise eligible under the Act. 

In processing applications from development credit corporations, it will be necessary to review the articles of incorporation and the by-laws to determine whether said articles comply with the requirements of our Loan Policy Statement. 

Government Procurement Amount of Awards to Small Business Increases 

The total value of new procurement under military prime contracts awarded during the first 6 months of fiscal year 1956 was $8,038 million compared with $7, 967 million during the first 6 months of fiscal 1955. Awards to small business firms increased to $1,414 million from $1, 268 million. As a result, the percentage of contract awards to small business increased to 17. 6 from 15. 7 per cent. 

Likewise, the net value of military prime contracts awarded showed an increase for both total procurement and awards to small business. The net value of total military prime contracts awarded was $6, 967 million compared with $6, 107 million; small business awards increased to $1, 383 million from $1,222 million. 

The small business percentages were 19. 8 and 20. 0, respectively. 

The trend of military procurement beginning with the first quarter of fiscal year 1954 through the second quarter of fiscal year 1956 is shown on chart 7. The trend of new procurement is compared with that of net procurement for both total contracts awarded and awards to small business.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION The term 

"New procurement" represents the sum of debit procurement actions; that is, new contracts plus contract changes that increase the amount of obligations by $10,000 or more. "cancellations" refers to the sum of credit procurement actions; that is, contract modifications that decrease the amount of obligations by $10,000 or more. "Net procurement" is used to refer to the net change in the amount of obligations resulting from the debit and credit procurement actions recorded during the period. 

These data seem to suggest that the small business share of new military prime contract awards increased. On a net value basis there was little change. However, it should be kept in mind that it is not safe to draw any firm conclusion from figures for a short period of time, such as 6 months. For such a short period, "the percentage of small business awards/ depends mainly on the types of commodities being procured in that particular period. 

When procurement of soft goods and easy-to-make items is a large part of the total, the small business percentage will be high. 

(Office of Assistant Secretary of Defense, Supply and Logistics)

As shown on page 24, military contracts awarded to small business during fiscal year 1955 totaled $3, 331 million compared with $3,076 million in the preceding year. However, since total new military procurement in fiscal year 1955 was $18, 530 million, compared with only $16, 843 million in the preceding year, the small business percentage of awards in 1955 was 18.0 per cent, slightly lower than the 18. 3 per cent in fiscal 1954, as shown in chart 8 on page 22.

Cancellations in 1955 were $3, 778 million compared with $5, 280 million in 1954. These cancellations were relatively greater for large businesses. Therefore, the smaller amount of cancellations of contracts with large firms in fiscal 1955 decreased the small business percentage of net procurement to 21. 8 from 25. 1 per cent.

Nevertheless, the net value of awards to small business increased to $3,214 million from $2,901 million as shown in chart 8 on page 22. 

For purposes of comparing the share of prime contracts awarded to small business in successive periods, the figures on new procurement provide the better basis for comparison. For the purposes of evaluating the share of defense dollars going to small business over a long period of time, the net procurement figures are to be preferred.

Advertised and Negotiated Military Procurement The small business share of advertised net procurement was greater in fiscal year 1955 than 1954. On a net value basis, advertised awards to small business increased to $1, 501 million from $1,150 million -- a slight increase to 10. 2 from 10. 0 per cent of total contracts awarded. The amount of negotiated small business awards decreased to $1,713 million from $1,751 million. 

As a result, the negotiated awards to small business decreased to 11. 6 from 15.1 per cent of total military procurement.

Total negotiated procurement decreased slightly to 83.8 from 84.5 per cent of total procurement.

Total military awards were distributed as follows: Small business received 10. 2 per cent of the total on an advertised basis and 11.6 per cent on a negotiated basis, whereas large business received 6.0 and 72. 2 per cent, respectively, as shown in chart 9 on page 23.

Small business obtains a relatively larger share of advertised than of negotiated procurement. This is primarily because a much higher proportion of what is advertised is potentially suitable for small business. In fiscal year 1955, 84.8 per cent of advertised military procurement was suitable for small business but only 21.2 per cent of negotiated procurement. Of the 84.8 per cent of suitable advertised procurement, small business received three-fourths and of the 21.2 per cent of the suitable negotiated procurement, small business received two-thirds. 

In fiscal year 1955, the amount of negotiated procurement potentially suitable for small business was $2,618 million. Of this amount, small business received $1,713 million. For advertised procurement, the amounts were $2, 024 million and $1, 501 million, respectively.

Procurement by Military Services 

Of the three military services, the Army awards the largest amount of prime contracts to small business, with the Navy and Air Force following in that order. The distribution of the 18 per cent of total new military procurement which went to small business in fiscal year 1955 was as follows: Army, 10.3 per cent; 

Navy, 4.2 per cent; Air Force, 3. 2 per cent; and Armed Services 

Petroleum Purchasing Agency, three-tenths of 1 per cent.

In terms of dollars, the total of $3, 331 million of contracts awarded to small business was divided as follows: Army, $1,904 million; Navy, $776 million; Air Force, $595 million; and Armed 

Services Petroleum Purchasing Agency, $56 million.

As illustrated by chart 10 on page 27, the volume of contracts awarded to small business by the Army and Air Force in fiscal year 1955 increased over awards a year earlier. As mentioned above, there was some decrease in the small business percentage of total procurement in fiscal year 1955 compared with the previous year. 

However, the small business percentage of Army new procurement was a little higher.

It is not possible either to evaluate satisfactorily the relative effectiveness of the programs as between departments or as between different time periods for one department. This is because what the military services buy varies both as to items and volume of procurement. Product mix and other factors obscure the effect of the departmental small business programs on small business participation.

Procurement by Programs 

Procurement actions of less than $10, 000 account for between 20 and 25 per cent of all the military prime contracts awarded to small business. The annual amounts for the past 3 fiscal years are indicated in chart 11, as shown on page 29. During fiscal year 1955, the net value of these less-than-$10,000 awards was $759, 000, 000, or 23.6 per cent of awards to small business. 

Procurement actions of $10,000 or more have been tabulated by program.

Construction, services and subsistence programs, together with actions of less than $10,000, account for two-thirds of total small business awards. The remaining one-third of awards includes hard goods, petroleum and textiles. The small business share of hard goods increased to 14.2 per cent in fiscal year 1955 compared with 12. 2 per cent a year earlier.

The small business percentages, by program, of new military procurement in fiscal year 1955 are presented in chart 12. 

Both the percentages and amounts of small business participation in defense mobilization are substantial for construction, construction equipment and subsistence. 

Small business probably participates through subcontracts awarded by prime contractors relatively more for hard than for soft goods. Even though adequate statistics are not available to show the amounts, it is reasonable to conclude that the sum of the prime contracts awarded to small business for hard goods plus subcontracts of this type would show that small business makes a significant and an important contribution.

Surplus Labor Areas 

Military supply contracts placed in surplus labor areas were much higher in fiscal 1955 than a year earlier. The increase was to $1,466 million from $557 million. While the amount placed as a result of preferential treatment for these areas and industries has been small compared with the total, it increased to $48 million from a little more than $8 million. Preference in the placing of these contracts is in accordance with Defense Manpower Policy No. 

4 issued by the Office of Defense Mobilization on November 4, 1953, to increase contracts and facilities in these areas.

Atomic Energy Commission 

The small business share of prime contract actions by the Atomic Energy Commission is quite small compared with subcontract actions by the agency's prime contractors. During the fiscal years 

1953-55, small business received prime contract awards totaling $136 million. This is only 2. 5 per cent of total Atomic Energy Commission awards of $5, 344 million. Awards for construction make up a large proportion of the agency's total prime contract awards. The second most important category has been services, other than utility services. 

The small business share of subcontract actions by Atomic Energy Commission cost-type prime contractors during this same period, 1953-55, was $707 million, or 42. 8 per cent of total subcontract actions of $1,650 million. The small business share of actions of this type is comprised mainly of awards for materials, supplies and equipment for construction and other purposes. 

The small business share of Atomic Energy Commission prime contract awards ($136 million) and subcontract awards ($707 million) totaled $843 million, or 15.8 per cent of total prime contract awards of $5, 344 million.

During this 3-year fiscal period, there was a downward trend both in total subcontract dollars resulting from Atomic Energy Commission prime contracts and in the small business share of the subcontract dollars. CHAPTER III FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE 

During the 6-months period July 1 to December 31, 1955, the Small Business Administration approved 625 business loans in the gross amount of $30, 332, 390 to assist small business firms. In the Disaster Loan Program, the Agency approved 

2, 056 loans aggregating $29, 290, 391 to assist business and home owners. Business loan approvals were 20 per cent greater, and disaster loan approvals 753 per cent greater than in the previous 

6-months period.

To date the Small Business Administration has approved 

2, 270 business loans for a gross amount of $114, 045, 903, and 

3, 299 disaster loans for a total of $37,904, 978.

The Agency's Financial Assistance Program is carried on in close co-operation with the banks of the Nation. As evidence of this, more than 66 per cent of the Agency's business loans have been in participation with banks. 

By participating in a Small Business Administration loan, a bank maintains a close relationship with its customer, the small business owner. At the same time, the borrower is given the opportunity to build a credit record with the bank. After the small firm has repaid the loan, the bank has a firmer basis for extending credit to it in the future, possibly without enlisting the aid of the Government. Thus, the bank participation-type loan is of broader assistance to the small business than a direct Government loan.

Of interest to the taxpayer is the fact that in a majority of the participation loans the Agency's participation is on a deferred basis, and the Government funds committed probably never will be withdrawn from the Treasury. In contrast, immediate participation and direct loans require immediate disbursement of Federal funds. 

The Agency is required to pay interest to the Treasury on funds withdrawn from the revolving fund at 2-5/8 per cent for the present fiscal year. 35

Congress has appropriated $105 million for the Small Business Administration revolving fund for lending purposes. As previously stated, business loans have been approved for a gross amount of $114, 045, 903 and disaster loans for a gross amount of $37, 904, 978. These totals exceed the $105 million appropriated to the revolving fund because a portion of the amount approved is the banks' share of participations; on deferred participation loans, the Small Business Administration's commitment liability for its share thereof is established at less than 100 per cent, based on the probability, as stated above, that in most cases Government funds never will be required to be withdrawn from the Treasury; loan repayments are available for additional loans; and some approved loans have been canceled at the borrowers' request prior to disbursement. In addition, the Agency has adopted a policy of offering for sale all of its direct loans as soon as they become seasoned. Funds realized from this source are also used to replenish the revolving fund.

A frequent reason for cancellation is that the small firm has been able to obtain bank financing based largely on the fact that the Small Business Administration had found a workable basis for making a loan. 

Financial Counseling 

Small businesses which encounter financial difficulties generally think first of borrowing funds, failing to recognize other possible courses of action which would eliminate the need for incurring debt. As a general rule, these small enterprises are not financially able to employ specialists in financial management. The Small Business Administration assists these firms through its Financial Counseling Program. 

Often a small firm's problem is under capitalization, or perhaps a need for liquidation of inventory or obsolete equipment, revision of credit policies or a change in product pricing principles. 

In other instances, a contemplated expansion may not be justified, the firm's product line may be too broad or too limited, or its sales policies may need review. These are a few of the possible factors considered by the Agency's specialists in counseling small businesses which have financial problems. 

Where a firm needs intermediate or long-term credit or short-term working capital for general business purposes or normal

growth, the Small Business Administration often can help it prepare a more effective request for funds to a prospective investor or private financial institution. 

By providing advice and counsel on financial problems, assisting in transactions with private lending institutions, and giving other specialized services, the Small Business Administration has been able to assist many small firms. Exactly how many have benefited from the program is not known since the results of any advisory service are difficult to measure. However, results are known in a sufficient number of cases to indicate that the program has had far-reaching benefits. Lending Program The lending program of the Small Business Administration is divided into two main parts as set forth in sections 207(a) and 207(b) of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended in 1955. 

Section 207(a) of the Act gives the Small Business 

Administration authority to make loans to small firms when credit is not otherwise available to them on reasonable terms. The loans are intended to fill a gap in the financing provided small firms by private financial institutions and are designed to stimulate and preserve the initiative, independence and enterprise of small businesses. To the greatest extent possible, and in keeping with the requirements of the Small Business Act, the Small Business Administration seeks to provide credit to small concerns in cooperation with private lending institutions. Its success in this is evidenced by the fact that about two-thirds of all its business loans are made in participation with banks and other private financial institutions.

Under the 1955 amendments to the Small Business Act, three significant changes were made in the Small Business Administration's business loan authority. The maximum amount of the Agency's portion of a business loan was increased from $150,000 to $250,000; it was specified that Agency loans shall bear interest at the rate prevailing in the area where the money is to be used, but not in excess of 6 per cent per annum; and authority was given the Agency to make loans to corporations formed and capitalized by groups of small business concerns for the purpose of establishing facilities to produce or secure raw materials or supplies.

Under this latter program, the maximum Agency loan is $250,000 multiplied by the number of separate small businesses which have formed and capitalized the corporation. 

In participation loans, the Agency has adopted the policy of permitting the bank to set the rate of interest on such loans as long as it does not exceed 6 per cent. The assumption here is that the bank will set the rate prevailing in the area where the money is loaned. This was considered the most practical procedure since it would be difficult for the Agency to establish standard rates differing in designated areas when rates of interest charged by private lending institutions vary within an area between individual banks and for different types of borrowers in such banks. 

There has been considerable interest in the loans to corporations formed and capitalized by groups of small business concerns. However, only one such loan application has been actually filed and processed. That loan in the amount of $700, 000, in participation with a bank in Maine, was approved during this semiannual period. The borrowing company is a corporation comprised of 19 small businesses engaged in the manufacture of potato starch. The proceeds of the loan will enable these small manufacturers to purchase potatoes and raw materials for the production of starch. The loan is adequately collateralized by the pledge of the finished product.

In the administration of its Business Loan Program, the Small Business Administration is governed by the policies established by the Agency's Loan Policy Board and the requirement of the Small Business Act that loans "shall be of such sound value or so secured as reasonably to assure repayment." The 

Agency applies the most liberal interpretation of these policies and requirements possible, consistent with sound credit principles, but at the same time is fully aware of its responsibilities to protect Government funds. Each loan application is thoroughly analyzed and full consideration given to all factors concerned. The Agency's lending program is under continual study and the area in which credit can be provided safely is being constantly reviewed. 

Limited Loan Participation Program 

The Small Business Administration recently has put into effect a Limited Loan Participation Program, which is designed especially to assist small retailers, wholesale distributors and service establishments.

As a rule, small concerns in these fields have little in the way of tangible collateral which they can pledge for a loan. 

However, they often have a good earnings record, competent management and a creditable record with local banks for meeting their obligations. They are frequently in need of term loans to modernize their facilities, expand their businesses or for other purposes.

Under this plan, the Small Business Administration will participate with a bank in a loan to a small retailer, wholesaler, service establishment or other business up to a maximum of $15,000 or 75 per cent of the total amount of the loan, whichever is the lesser. Generally, the participating bank's share in the loan must represent additional exposure on the part of the bank equal to not less than 25 per cent of the total amount of the loan. However, if the bank has outstanding a short-term loan to the borrower or a longer term loan on which repayments have been made promptly as originally scheduled, this loan may be substituted for the new money requirement or included in computing the bank's participation in the loan. In either case, the bank's share in the loan must be equal to at least the outstanding loan to be refunded with a part of the new loan or 25 per cent of the total amount of the full loan, whichever is the larger. 

The maximum maturity on such participation loans is 5 years with a monthly repayment schedule, including interest at the rate of not more than 6 per cent per annum. 

The Small Business Administration believes that through this plan banks will be able to meet the financial needs of a considerably greater number of small business, concerns than heretofore has been possible. 

The program was operative on a trial basis during the last few months of 1955. During this period, 47 loans with a gross amount of $534, 715 were approved. Of this amount the banks' share was $184, 737 and the Small Business Administration's share was $349, 978. It is too early as yet to tell precisely what the effect of this program will be.

make loans to assist owners of homes and businesses which have been damaged or destroyed by storms, floods or other natural disasters. Loans can be made only to the extent of actual losses not covered by insur- ance.

A summary of the application activity under the Disaster Loan 

Program through December 

1955 is shown in chart 13. 

Approximately 71 per cent of applications received and 91 per cent of the amount requested originated as a result of losses to business firms.

Applications from homes damaged by disasters constituted the remaining 29 per cent of the total applications requesting about $6 million.

The 1955 amendment to the Act also gave the Agency authority to make loans to businesses within an area where a drought is occurring and which have suffered economic loss as a result of the drought. These loans are limited to businesses within an area designated as a drought disaster area by the President or the Secretary of Agriculture. 

To date, there has been only moderate interest in drought loans, although the program has been widely publicized in the drought areas. The principal difficulty experienced in the administration of the program has been in establishing the fact that the need for the loan is attributable to the drought as well as the extent of such loss.

Since disaster loans are intended as a rehabilitation measure, much more liberal credit standards are applied to them than would be prudent for the Agency's business loans. 

Interest rates, too, are lower. Under the Small Business Act the rate cannot exceed 3 per cent per annum. 

The past 6-months period has been the most active of the Small Business Administration insofar as disaster loans are concerned. 

During August 1955, a series of hurricanes struck the East Coast first in the Carolinas, then in a large portion of New England. The Small Business Administration declared disaster areas which included the entire State of Connecticut, 3 counties in Delaware, 9 counties in Massachusetts, 12 counties in New Jersey, 6 counties in New York, 27 counties in Pennsylvania and 1 county in Rhode Island.

To meet the needs of hurricane victims, the Agency established 21 emergency disaster loan offices in the New England area and 2 offices in the Carolinas. Personnel were detailed to these offices from Agency regional offices; in addition, banks in the area assigned some 63 credit people to assist in the work at no cost to the Government. To speed processing of loan applications, the Agency also increased the authority of local offices to approve disaster loans and simplified the application form for small disaster home loans.

Washington 

12-8-55 Washington Flood 12-12-55 2 California 11 12-30-55 Nevada 12-30-55 Oregon 11 12-30-55 Because of the scope of these disasters, it became apparent that the statutory limitation of $25, 000, 000 on outstanding disaster loans of the Agency would be inadequate if all eligible loan applicants were to be given assistance. After consultation with 

Congressional leaders and members of the White House staff, Agency officials decided that until Congress could increase this limitation, the Small Business Administration would make loans to qualifying businesses in disaster areas as regular business loans

and at the 6 per cent business loan interest rate. A clause was inserted in the loan authorization stating that the interest rate would revert to 3 per cent at such time as Congressional action permitted.

It should be noted that this procedure would not cause financial hardship to borrowers, as Congressional leaders indicated that action to lift the legal limit of $25, 000, 000 on disaster loans would undoubtedly take place prior to the time that the first payments on the loans would be required. 

At the time this emergency method was adopted, less than $5, 000, 000 of the $25, 000, 000 for Small Business Administration disaster loans remained uncommitted. It therefore was necessary for the Agency to allocate this balance to the various disaster areas. By approving all possible applications under the business loan authority and by making frequent adjustments in the fund allocations, the Agency hoped to be able to avoid refusing any applications for lack of legislative authority. As of January 6, 1956, only $240, 000 remained available for new applications. 

As stated previously, through December 31, 1955, the 

Small Business Administration had approved 3, 299 disaster loans. 

Of these, 2,755 were 3 per cent disaster loans totaling $27, 471, 412 and 544 were processed as business loans under section 207(a) of the Small Business Act, for a total of $10, 433, 566. It should be pointed out that these approval figures are cumulative and exceed the dollar amount of loans outstanding by reason of subsequent cancellations and repayments on loans. In addition, the Small 

Business Administration processed seven loans, amounting to $4,306, 000, which were approved by the Treasury Department under section 302 of the Defense Production Act. 

The problem of aiding disaster victims grew even more acute when floods struck in California, Oregon and Nevada in the latter part of December, causing extensive damage to homes and businesses. While accurate estimates cannot yet be made, it is possible that the Agency may receive disaster loan applications totaling as much as $15, 000, 000. 

To meet this new emergency, the Small Business

Administration established 6 temporary disaster loan offices in 

California, 3 in Oregon, and 1 in Nevada.

Bank participation in loans has been of great help to the Agency in providing financial assistance quickly to disaster victims. Banks have been very co-operative in processing loan applications, thus lightening the Agency's own workload. In addition, many loan applicants prefer to deal with their own banks, since the banks know their financial condition, are familiar with their business, and may be able to fill other financial needs during the course of the loan. 

The Small Business Administration has approved 94 

loans in conjunction with banks in this program. In many cases, where house loans are involved, banks have charged the same interest rate 3 percent that the Agency charges on its portion of the loan. To encourage this, the Agency's Loan Policy 

Board has agreed in these cases not to charge the bank any fee for Agency participation in the loan. 

Loan Applications 

During the period July 1-December 31, 1955, the 

Small Business Administration received 1, 503 applications for business loans. The number of applications reached a high of 

280 in the month of September and ranged from 205 to 273 in the other months of the period. During the 6-months period covered in the previous semiannual report (January 1-June 30, 1955), applications reached a high of 319 in March 1955 and were between 224 and 277 in the other months of the period, except in 

January 1955, when they were 204.

Because of widespread disasters, activity in the Disaster Loan Program increased greatly during this reporting period. The Agency received 2, 571 disaster loan applications in contrast to 300 during the previous 6 months.

Approvals and Declines 

A report of business loan application activity from the start of the Agency's lending program in October 1953 through December 31, 1955, is shown on page 46. The total number of applications received was 7, 110. Of this number, 2, 270 were approved 538 by regional offices and 1,732 by the Washington office. The total number of applications withdrawn before processing was 1,097. In a large number of cases where applications were withdrawn, financing had been obtained from private sources

Loan Declinations Loan declinations by the Small Business Administration for the last 6-months period are shown on page 51. The most frequent reason for declining applications was that earning ability was not demonstrated or assured for the future. 

In many instances, applications are denied because of insufficient collateral. Other reasons for declinations include insufficient equity investment, need for Small Business Administration funds not demonstrated, and unsatisfactory financial condition.

The average size of loans on a cumulative basis on December 31, 1955, was $50, 240, compared with an average size of $50, 890 at the end of the previous reporting period and an average size of $52, 943 on December 31, 1954. While the average size of loans on a cumulative basis is $50, 240, for the last 6-months period it was only $48, 532. Thus, the average size of loans has been steadily diminishing.

It is interesting to note that loans of over $150,000 constituted only 1.9 per cent of the total number on December 31, 1954, and only 2 per cent on December 31, 1955.

During the last 6 months, there has been a rather significant change in the distribution of loans on a size basis. 

The percentage of number of loans under $10, 000 increased during the period to 22. 9, compared with a cumulative percentage of 16. 2. At the other end of the scale, the percentage of loans exceeding $150, 000 increased during the period to 3. 7 compared with the cumulative percentage of 2. 0.

The increase in the proportion of loans under $10,000 has been due partly to the Agency's Limited Loan Participation Program. The increase in the proportion of loans exceeding

$150,000 has been due to the increase in the Agency's maximum loan limit from $150, 000 to $250,000. This increased number and proportion of loans exceeding $150, 000 increased their per cent of the total amount to 18.5 per cent compared with a cumulative per cent of 8. 8. Thus, the Business Loan Program is expanding in both directions, which should increase the number of applications for loans.

Loans By Types of Industries 

As previously indicated, there has been an increase in the proportion of loans approved for amounts under $10,000. 

Further, the Agency is now making a higher proportion of these small loans to firms in the nonmanufacturing industries. Of the business loans approved during the 6-months period July 1December 31, 1955, there were 279 to manufacturers and 346 to other types of concerns. Further, 260 of the 346 were to small businesses engaged in the wholesale and retail trades and in providing services. 

Of the 2, 270 business loans for $114, 045, 903 approved through December 31, 1955, a total of 1, 127 amounting to $65, 407, 004 were to manufacturers. The remaining 1, 143 loans amounting to $48, 638, 899 were to wholesale, retail and other nonmanufacturing industries. 

Use of Proceeds

An analysis of the use of proceeds of loans during the past 6 months indicates a shift to the use of proceeds to purchase relatively more equipment and to consolidate obligations rather than to increase working capital or to construct new'facilities. 

A summary of the proposed use of proceeds of business loans approved through December 31, 1955, is shown on page 57. 

As indicated, two-fifths of the funds were intended to increase working capital, one-fourth to construct facilities, and one-tenth to purchase equipment. The remaining one-fourth were intended for use in consolidating obligations.

Disbursements and Commitments As of December 31, 1955, disbursements had been initiated on 1, 437 of the 1, 829 net approved business loans -approximately 79 per cent of net approvals. Disbursements had not yet been started on 392 business loans because: 1. 2. 3. 

Some borrowers had not yet complied with the terms and conditions of loan approval. 

In many instances where Small Business Administration loans were for construction purposes, interim financing was available to the small firm.

In a number of cases, borrowers had requested that disbursement be delayed pending the possibility of working out private financing.

As of December 31, 1955, disbursements made by banks or the Small Business Administration amounted to $67,718,647, out of a total net commitment to borrowers of $91, 580, 264. The Small Business Administration's share of the $91, 580, 264 of net commitment was $76, 164, 342.

In the case of disaster loans, net commitments to borrowers were $35, 223, 297. The Small Business Administration share was $34, 460, 747. Consequently, the gross amount of net commitments to all borrowers was $126, 803, 560, of which the 

Small Business Administration share was $110, 625, 089.

In the case of disaster loans, disbursements totaled $22, 403,000 out of total net commitments of $35, 223, 000. Consequently, total commitments to all borrowers equaled $126, 803, 560. 

Of this amount, the Small Business Administration's share was $110, 625, 089.

Sale of Loans 

The sale of loans program was initiated by the Agency almost a year ago and to date 25 loans, approved in the amount of $1,236, 200, have been sold or refinanced through banks. No loan is offered for sale unless it has been disbursed for a 6-months period or longer. In all cases the borrower's bank of deposit is initially contacted to see if it is interested in purchasing the loan. 

This is in line with our general policy of trying to be of assistance to the small business concern in establishing a regular line of credit with its bank of deposit and also to assist it in maintaining normal bank-customer relationship. Since this is a continuing program, the Agency is presently offering through its regional offices 300 small business loans in the total dollar amount of approximately $12, 800, 000 for sale to private lending institutions. 

Therefore, there can be no question but that the Agency is complying with the intent of the legislation not to compete with private lending institutions.

Cancellations 

Of the 2, 270 business loans approved since the inception of the Small Business Administration, 441 have been canceled in full prior to disbursement. The principal reasons for cancellations have been: 1. 2. 3. 4. 

Borrower made other arrangements for funds. 

Borrower determined he could get along without the loan. 

Borrower was unable or unwilling to comply with loan conditions.

Borrower's financial condition deteriorated to the extent that loan could not be disbursed.

Delinquencies 

As of December 31, 1955 disbursements had been made on 1, 437 business loans in a total amount of $67,718, 647. 

Of this amount, borrowers were delinquent in excess of 60 days for a total amount of $1, 173, 405, or approximately 1.7 per cent of the total disbursed. For the total disbursements of $22, 403, 292 on disaster loans, borrowers were delinquent for only $19,907, or slightly less than one-tenth of 1 per cent. 

It is expected that many of the present delinquencies will be brought to a current position without loss to this Agency.

CHAPTER IV GOVERNMENT PROCUREMENT 

Significant progress was made in broadening the program under which the Small Business Administration and other Government agencies act co-operatively to increase the participation of small business in Government procurement. 

In the area of military purchasing three Department of Defense directives, one of which was issued during the last 6 months, have been coordinated with the Small Business Adminis tration and released to provide increased opportunities for small business concerns to obtain defense business. These are: 1. 

DOD Directive 4100.9, revised November 14, 

1955 This directive, when implemented by the Army, Navy and Air Force, will provide the means for a greatly improved and expanded 

Small Business Set-Aside Program. The 

Navy implementation was the first to be completed. 

2. DOD Directive 4100. 10, dated December 16, 1954 - This directive covers the over-all operation of the Department of Defense Small 

Business Program and provides for the coordination with the Small Business Administration of certain functions to assist small business firms. 

3. DOD Directive 4100. 20, dated April 19, 1955 

This directive provides for establishing and conducting a "Defense Subcontracting Small 

Business Program" with prime contractors receiving defense contracts in excess of $1,000,000, and for a system of obtaining information concerning the amount subcontracted by these contractors. The reporting system is still being developed. 61

In the field of civilian agency purchasing, small business set-aside programs have been established with the General Services Administration, largest single civilian buying agency of the Government, the Veterans Administration, and the Department of Agriculture. 

Negotiations are nearing completion with the Post Office and Interior Departments for establishment of similar programs. 

Since July 1, 1955, the number and value of Government purchases reserved for competitive award to small business have increased substantially. During this period, more than 2, 100 purchases amounting to approximately $192, 800, 000 were earmarked for competitive award to small firms. Other suitable procurements totaling more than $20, 000, 000 could not be restricted to small business because they were set aside for award to firms located in disaster areas.

There has been a definite increase in the interest and co-operation of the Department of Defense in making awards to small concerns.

Good progress also has been made in the Small Business Administration's program of counseling small firms on selling to the Government and advising them of specific bidding opportunities. 

In the last 6 months, the Agency has made more than 50, 000 notifications of prime contract opportunities to small firms. 

Other Agency programs and services also have served to increase small business competition in many areas of Government buying. 

Joint Determination Program 

This program is conducted by the Small Business Adminis tration in co-operation with the military departments, the General 

Services Administration, Veterans Administration and Department of Agriculture. Its purpose is to help small business achieve participation in Federal buying by reserving for competitive bidding or negotiation among small firms all or part of individual Government purchases.

To carry out this program the Small Business Administration has assigned representatives to principal purchasing centers.

These representatives screen proposed purchases and advise procurement officials of those which they believe should be earmarked, in whole or in part, for award exclusively to small firms. 

Each such Small Business Administration request that all or part of a procurement be reserved for small business is reviewed by the procurement officials and either agreed to, modified or rejected.

The purchasing centers to which Agency representatives are assigned are listed in Appendix B. 

During the period July-December 1955, approximately half of the joint determinations proposed by the Small Business Administration were on Army procurements. 

Of 2, 276 joint determinations initiated by the Agency, 1, 137 were on proposed Army purchases. The estimated value of these Army procure- $105 million was almost half the estimated value of ments 

all joint determinations initiated by the Agency. 

During the 6-months period, 2, 028 procurements were awarded to small business concerns as a result of the joint determination program.

These actually resulted in 3, 705 contract awards to small firms.

The number and dollar volume of joint determinations from July through December 1955 are shown by military service and the General Services Administration in the table on page 64. 

There has been a gratifying increase in the number and value of procurements reserved for award to small business. The number of joint determinations initiated by the Small Business Administration in this reporting period increased to 2, 276, as compared with 1, 532 for the same period a year earlier and 733 for the period ending December 1953. The increase in the number of joint determinations agreed to was similar. Chart 19 shows the dollar value of the set-aside procurements and contract awards for each of these periods. The number of procurements awarded to small firms increased even more sharply to 2, 028 compared with 854 for the period ending December 1954 and 256 as of 

December 1953. Total contract awards to small business are discussed in the Government Procurement section of chapter II,
