An Improved Future Program 

To small firms which wish to participate in military purchasing, the issuance on November 14 of an amended Department of Defense instruction concerning the Joint Determination Program was of particular significance. This instruction, directed to all military purchasing offices, broadens the Small Business 

Administration-Department of Defense joint program of earmarking procurements for exclusive award to small firms. When implemented by the Army, Navy and Air Force, the new program should increase substantially the opportunity for small firms to sell to military installations. 

The main features of this enlarged and improved program between the Small Business Administration and the Department of 

Defense are:

1. 

It permits the screening of procurements of under $10,000 for possible restriction to small business, when desirable. 

Previously, screening was limited to purchases amounting to $10,000 and over. 

During fiscal year 1955, the value of new Defense Department procurement actions with business firms for work in the United States was $18, 530 million. The value of purchases screened by 

Small Business Administration representatives was $5, 465 million. 

Under the new Defense Department instruction the amount screened should increase sharply. 2. 3. 

The instruction directs (rather than permits, as previously) that entire purchases be reserved for award to small business when this is in the interest of maintaining or mobilizing the Nation's full productive capacity or in the interest of war or national defense.

It directs (rather than permits) that part of a purchase be reserved for small business when the entire purchase cannot be earmarked for small business and when the following conditions exist: a. b. 

Quantities can be divided into two or more economical production runs, and Two or more small business sources are known or believed to have the ability to supply the portion being reserved for small business.

The new instruction also directs that the portion set aside for small business shall be such as to make maximum use of small business capacity. 4. 

The instruction permits the screening of all procurements without exception including negotiated as well as advertised

procurements.

Formerly, the Small 

Business Administration was excluded from screening emergency, research and development, and classified procurements. 

5. The instruction directs that procurement information for the Small Business 

Administration referral program 

(referrals of bid opportunities to small firms) be made available to the Agency on request with no limitations or exceptions. Under the former agreement, information furnished by the military was chiefly on purchases covered by joint determinations.

Considerable progress also has been made in expanding the Joint Determination Program to include additional military purchasing offices. Since July 1, 1955, the Agency has begun making joint determinations at the following additional installations:

Quartermaster Research and Development Command, 

Natick, Massachusetts 

Red River Arsenal, Texarkana, Texas 

Fort Hood, Texas

U. S. Naval Air Station, Memphis, Tennessee England Air Force Base, Alexandria, Louisiana 

Gadsden Air Force Depot, Gadsden, Alabama

Small Business Administration regional offices also are contacting other Government purchasing installations in their areas to determine the feasibility of expanding the Joint Determination Program to these installations. Expansion of the program to purchasing centers throughout the United States is believed essential to increased small business participation in Government procurement. 

It is particularly necessary as military items become more and more complex and large firms tend to move into fields formerly supplied principally by small business. 

Another significant development during this period was the establishment of a Small Business Administration Joint Determination 

Program with three civilian procurement agencies of the Government-- 

Veterans Administration, Department of Agriculture, and the General Services Administration.

This program, begun on a trial basis at the General Services Administration's New York office in September, already has resulted in purchases amounting to $1,310, 133 being reserved for competition among small firms. Procedures are now being developed for screening of procurements, by Small Business Adminis tration representatives, at the National Buying Division of the 

General Services Administration in Washington. 

Negotiations also are underway with the Department of the Interior and Post Office Department for establishment of similar joint determination programs.

The Small Business 

Administration believes that these programs will be established in the near future.

Other Agency Activities at Purchasing Centers 

The earmarking of suitable purchases for exclusive award to small firms is the major activity of Small Business Administration representatives at procurement centers. In addition, however, these Agency representatives assist small business concerns in many other ways. For example, they help individual small firms obtain the opportunity to bid on specific purchases and help them get on proper bid lists; give them general advice and assistance in selling to the Government; assist them in presenting grievances to purchasing officials; and serve to offset, for small business, the tremendous advantage enjoyed by large firms whose agents regularly visit the buying installations. Small Business 

Administration representatives also serve as spokesmen for small business in top-level conferences at the purchasing centers. 

Broadening the Area of Participation by Small Business A main objective of the Small Business Administration is to increase participation by small business in those Government purchases which at present involve only a limited number of suppliers. 

One way for the Agency to do this is by recommending capable small concerns to compete on purchases which at present are supplied chiefly by large firms. Small Business Administration representatives at purchasing centers therefore ask the Agency's field offices for names of competent small firms whenever there is insufficient small business competition, or a limited number of suppliers, on a particular purchase.

By means of inventories of small plant facilities in their areas, Small Business Administration regional offices locate capable

small firms which desire to bid on the items for which additional competition is sought. The Agency representatives at the purchasing centers then use names of these firms to supplement the small business portion of the bidders lists. 

Since July 1, 1954, Small Business Administration regional offices have furnished 6, 709 small business sources of supply to procurement offices in need of additional competition on their purchases; 

2,077 of these sources have been supplied in the past 6 months.

Since this program is now being given higher priority by the Agency, the number of sources furnished purchasing offices is expected to increase rapidly.

Typical items for which additional small suppliers have been furnished to purchasing offices are: Steel drums; paint; household refrigerators; retrigeration storage in transit; cleaning compound; overhaul of aircraft and aircraft engines; aircraft hydraulic rings; portable battery chargers; tables and chairs; men's cotton, kneelength stockings; and oil-fired space heaters.

Some Small Business Administration regional directors also have arranged with purchasing installations in their areas to furnish names of capable small suppliers of items for which the purchasing offices need increased competition. For example, the 

Second Army Command, Fort George G. Meade, Maryland, asked the Small Business Administration's Baltimore Branch Office to furnish names of additional small suppliers of fixtures and equipment. The next contract for these items was awarded to one of the small sources furnished by the Agency. 

Small Business Administration field offices also have contacted purchasing officials of State, county and city governments to offer use of the Agency's facilities in obtaining greater small business competition on their purchases. The response from these officials has been good. Arrangements with local officials 

Federal, State, county and city to increase small business participation in their purchasing are especially helpful to very small firms.

Advertised Versus Negotiated Procurements 

In many instances, small firms fare better on formally advertised than on negotiated procurements. The Small Business 

Administration therefore encourages and assists contracting officers

to adopt firm specifications on their purchases, so that the formally advertised technique of buying can be used more widely. 

Contracting officers usually are anxious to obtain firm specifications, drawings and a "pilot model" in order to get the greatest possible competition. However, the rapid development of new items and improvement of existing items, particularly in the case of military purchasing, often hampers contracting officers, because firm specifications and drawings are not available until after the product has been tested by the purchasing agency. This gives the producer of the pilot model a competitive advantage which other firms later find difficult to overcome. Consequently, the 

Small Business Administration emphasizes to small firms the importance of competing for procurements involving the development of new items or improvement of established items. 

Experience indicates that the Joint Determination Program has been responsible for use of the advertising procedure in the case of many purchases which formerly were negotiated. This is especially true where only part of a purchase is reserved for award to small firms. Normally, this results in the unrestricted portion of the purchase being advertised, and the portion restricted to small business negotiated within the price range developed by advertising. 

Small Business Administration Liaison Officer with Department of Defense 

A Small Business Administration liaison officer is assigned to the Defense Department to help achieve greater coordination of the two agencies' small business programs. The Small Business Adminis tration representative participates in conferences and in the preparation of directives relating to small business policies of the Defense Department and the three military services. Defense officials have given this liaison officer excellent co-operation and his participation at the Defense Department's policy level has resulted in a number of changes beneficial to small business. An example of this is the recent strengthening of the agencies' Joint Determination Program. 

Procurement Assistance Counseling Program 

In its counseling program, the Small Business Administration helps small firms determine which items or services they can supply to the Government, and assists them in being included on the appropriate bidders lists. In addition, by agreement with

the Defense Department, the Agency may assist small business concerns in obtaining bid sets for any purchase which is suitable for supply by small business. 

Upon request, small firms can receive from the Small Business Administration notices of suitable bidding opportunities in both the prime and subcontract fields. Many Agency field offices also obtain bid sets from purchasing offices in their areas and display them for informational purposes. The offices inform interested firms as to how the bid sets and the specifications and drawings relating to the purchases, may be obtained or where they are available for inspection. In this connection, each Small Business Administration field office maintains a set of Government packing and packaging specifications especially selected to fill the needs of small firms. These sets are available to small business owners for reference purposes.

Here are a few typical cases and comments by the Agency's field offices regarding the Procurement Assistance 

Counseling Program: 

Baltimore "A representative of a newly organized precision machine company visited our Procurement Information Center and was counseled regarding sources of bidding opportunities. The businessman was amazed at the fund of information available here. He found five specific procurement opportunities, reviewed drawings at the Center, and submitted bids. We also referred the company to various prime contractors for possible subcontract work. " Minneapolis "A small Wisconsin firm had been trying for over a year to dispose of cap boxes which were overproduced on a subcontract. 

When it requested assistance from us, we coordinated the case with our Chicago Regional Office and the Agency's representative at Ordnance Ammunition Command, Joliet. As a result, the company obtained a contract for 1, 500 boxes and is being placed on the bidders list for future procurements of the item. The firm also was notified of another procurement for 200, 000 cap boxes on which it may receive the award. "'

Denver 

"A local firm which was unsuccessful in obtaining bid papers from a procurement center used our 'bulletin board' set, was the successful bidder, and was awarded a $120,000 contract. " 

Kansas City "More and more small firms report they are following our advice concerning getting on bidders lists. One Nebraska firm which did so was awarded a $222,000 Navy contract for mine anchors." Chicago "An electronic instruments manufacturer who suffered from lack of orders for several months has been materially assisted by two sizable commercial contracts obtained for him through the efforts of this office. "

In addition to providing information on selling to the Government, the Small Business Administration, through its regional and branch offices and its representatives in procurement centers, is called upon to assist small business concerns in many other ways.

For example, the Agency provides small firms with advice and guidance in regard to: 

1. Bidding technicalities in both advertised and negotiated procurements. 

2. Getting on specific bidders lists which lack an adequate number of small firms. 

3. Expediting inspections of pre-production samples of components or completed end items. 

4. Getting products accepted for inclusion on qualified products lists. 

5. Obtaining bid sets, prints and specifications. 

6. Securing performance bonds.

7. Obtaining progress payments or advance payments. 

8. Obtaining payment of invoices. 

9. Handling of claims. 

10. Being included in the mobilization base.

11. Tax amortization. 

The following are typical instances in which Small Business Administration representation in behalf of small firms was necessary: 

1. A small Boston manufacturer was low bidder on a purchase of generating sets. The purchasing office was reluctant to make the award to the firm, and accordingly had the procurement withdrawn. The Small Business Administration protested this action to the chief of the service involved and pressed the case until the Comptroller General ruled that the procurement should be reinstated. 

The award, amounting to $411,000, was then made to the small firm. 

Later, the Small Business Administration issued a certificate of competency to the firm which enabled it to obtain another contract, and, subsequently, it received two additional awards. These three contracts totaled $449, 000. The small concern states that without the assistance of the Small Business Administration, it would not have received a single award. 

2. The Small Business Administration's Atlanta Regional 

Office, acting on the protest of a small business whose low bid on a purchase was rejected, found that a clause in the invitation for bid was unduly restrictive. The clause served to limit eligibility to two large business concerns. The Agency was able to have the invitations canceled and the specifications rewritten to eliminate the previous restriction. The protesting small business is now in line for the award, at a saving of $30, 000 to the Government. 

3. A Baltimore lumber dealer was low bidder several times on purchases by a Federal civilian agency but failed to receive the awards. At the firm's request, a representative of the Small 

Business Administration visited the procurement agency and learned that the awards had been made to another company through clerical error. When the same mistake occurred on the next procurement on which the small firm was low bidder, the Small Business Administration again protested to the purchasing agency. This time the necessary correction was made.

4. A Massachusetts firm appealed to the Small Business

Administration because its low bid on a purchase had been declared nonresponsive, on the ground that it had not signed and included with the bid a later amendment to the invitation. Since the firm had not received the amendment until after the bid opening, the Small Business Administration interceded in its behalf. The company now has been notified that it will receive the award. 

5. A small firm in Philadelphia was assisted by the 

Agency's Philadelphia Regional Office to obtain a Government contract on which it had bid $120,000 below the next low bidder. 

This assistance not only helped the small firm but also brought a large saving to the Government. 

To illustrate the volume of procurement assistance cases handled by the Small Business Administration, during the past 6 months, the Agency's field offices have reported 9, 840 cases of counseling and 540 instances of representation to procurement officials in behalf of small firms. During this period, Small Business 

Administration representatives at purchasing installations have recommended 2, 776 firms to contracting officers for the opportunity to bid on specific purchases.

U. S. Government Purchasing Directory 

Of special importance to small business concerns is the publication of a revised and expanded edition of the "U. S. Government Purchasing Directory." The new directory is a complete revision of the one first published by the Small Business Adminis tration in March 1954. Copies are available to small firms from the Superintendent of Documents for 50 cents per copy. 

The purchasing directory tells who buys what and where in the Federal Government by a listing of the items and classes of items purchased by the military and civilian agencies of the Government, and a complementary listing of the offices which do the buying. 

The directory also provides small firms with varied information on selling to the Government and obtaining subcontracts. It is especially useful to small firms in learning which offices buy items they can furnish, so that they may obtain placement on the proper bidders lists. 

Much of this information was previously difficult for a small business to obtain. Since past experience has indicated that

closely held information tends to develop practices inimical to the integrity of the procurement system, it is the policy of the Small Business Administration to make information on Government procurement easily available directly to potential bidders and to disseminate such information as widely as possible. The purchasing directory is of great value in this regard. 

Copies of the purchasing directory have been furnished all Small Business Administration field offices, military small business specialists, and contracting officials of the Federal civilian agencies for their use in counseling small business firms. 

Prime Contract Referral Program 

A Small Business Administration service of much value to small firms is notification to them of procurement opportunities which are suitable to their facilities. In this program, the Small 

Business Administration field offices review bidding forms, drawings and specifications received from purchasing offices to determine whether or not small firms in their areas can supply the items. 

Firms whose facilities records, on file with the Small Business Administration, indicate that they can furnish the items are notified of the bidding opportunities by use of a standardized "Notice of a Government Purchase. " Since this Agency program gives them greater competition on their purchases, many Federal purchasing agencies are glad to co-operate by furnishing bid sets. 

The main objective of the Prime Contract Referral 

Program is to acquaint small firms with opportunities to bid on Government purchases until they have obtained listing on the appropriate bidders lists. Accordingly, at the time that it notifies a small firm of a contract opportunity, the Small Business Administration also urges it to request placement on the bidders list for the particular item or service. In addition, the Agency includes with the notice a leaflet which outlines important points in selling to the Government. Referrals which pertain to a specific item to be purchased by a specific purchasing office are discontinued after the small firm has had ample time to be placed on that office's bidders list.

In the past 18 months, Small Business Administration field offices have made more than 200, 000 notifications of prime contract opportunities to qualified small concerns in their areas. 

Of these, about 50, 000 notifications have been made in the last

6 months. Since July 1, 1954, Small Business Administration regional offices have reported that 5, 171 contract awards totaling $402, 833, 000 have been made to firms which were notified of the bidding opportunities by the Small Business Administration. 

It is believed that this figure is much understated since the award information is difficult to obtain. 

Examples of the effectiveness of the Prime Contract Referral Program are: 

1. An electronics research and manufacturing concern whose orders were falling off seriously, appealed to the Small 

Business Administration's Boston office for assistance. Agency staff members made a plant inspection to learn the specific nature of the firm's facilities and abilities. Over the next few weeks, several appropriate contract opportunities were referred to this company. On the fifth referral, the firm was awarded a $111,295 contract by the Bureau of Ships. This has alleviated the critical situation at the plant and kept its highly specialized facilities available for potential defense requirements. 

2. As a result of a referral by the Small Business 

Administration's Kansas City Regional Office, a Kansas City firm was awarded a contract amounting to more than $2,000,000. 

The Agency's regional office had received the drawings and specifications for the purchase from the Small Business Administration representative at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base. 

Subcontracting Program 

In recent months the Small Business Administration has received fewer complaints from small firms about their inability to obtain subcontracts. This may be due to the rising volume of non-Government business and to the effect of the subcontracting programs of the Small Business Administration, the Defense 

Department and other Federal agencies. When implemented, the new Department of Defense program, which provides for a system of obtaining statistics on the amount subcontracted by firms receiving contracts in excess of $1,000, 000, should result in more complete information regarding the amount of defense business subcontracted to small firms.

The Small Business Administration aids small firms seeking subcontracts by referring them to prime contractors or

major subcontractors that need complementary facilities. Agency representatives develop subcontracting opportunities for small firms by calling on prime contractors, emphasizing to them the benefits of subcontracting, and determining which items they may wish to subcontract. An effective subcontracting program is dependent upon such personal contacts with prime contractors in behalf of specific small firms and in regard to definite contracts. 

This Small Business Administration program ties in well with the Defense Department program whereby firms which receive prime contracts for more than $1,000, 000 are urged to designate a small business liaison officer and subcontract work to small firms. 

Small Business Administration field offices maintain close contact with these small business liaison officers to develop maximum subcontracting opportunities for firms registered with the Agency. As an example, when a large aircraft manufacturer recently obtained a $100, 000, 000 order for Navy jet planes, the firm was contacted immediately by a Small Business Administration representative, who was told that the contract would double, perhaps quadruple, this company's subcontracting needs. The Small Business Administration's 

Dallas office will co-operate closely with this company to provide every possible subcontracting opportunity to small firms. Small Business Administration field offices also continue to maintain liaison with the Atomic Energy Commission's operations contractors in order to provide small firms with information about the needs of these plants for maintenance, repair and operating supplies. 

Agency staff members have made 1, 432 contacts with prime contractors in the past 6 months. As a result of these contacts, the Agency made 1, 702 referrals of subcontracting opportunities to small firms. 

Typical statements from Small Business Administration field offices concerning the subcontracting program are: Baltimore "We are receiving an increased number of requests from prime contractors for small firms which provide anodizing service and have special technical abilities. Our subcontracting program with a large airplane manufacturer also is developing successfully. 

For instance, the firm has asked for and been furnished the names of small companies capable of large milling and profiling capacity.

Kansas City 

"We secured a subcontract for a small Kansas City aluminum foundry which was closed down because of inability to obtain primary aluminum ingot. The subcontract, on an Air Force rated order, will permit the firm to obtain 20,000 pounds of aluminum ingot and to resume operations."

Atlanta 

"A large contractor has been furnished facility information on 653 firms registered with our office. The firm will use the list for its own referrals of subcontracting opportunities." 

Small Business Administration Prime Contract Program 

The Small Business Administration is empowered to enter into contracts with any Department or Agency of the United States Government to furnish supplies and services, and to arrange for the performance of such contracts through subcontracting to small business concerns or others, as may be necessary to enable the Small Business Administration to perform such contracts. 

This authority, enacted into law during World War II, was deemed by Congress to be a useful tool in that time of national emergency because small firms were not able to get subcontract work sufficient to utilize their capacity. Congress re-enacted the authority for the Small Defense Plants Administration apparently on the logic that the period of national emergency required that the authority be reactivated.

The Small Business Administration holds the opinion that its authority to take prime contracts in order to extend subcontracting should continue on a stand-by basis for use in emergencies. 

Procurement Aid to Firms in Disaster Areas 

The Small Business Administration is co-operating with other Federal agencies in disseminating procurement information to firms located in disaster areas. As an example, the Navy 

Department and the Small Business Administration have arranged for bid sets on all unclassified Navy procurements to be sent to 

Small Business Administration field offices and to Navy Material 

Inspection Offices located in disaster areas. Small Business 

Administration offices make referrals of these procurements to

small firms registered with them, and also maintain the bid sets for inspection by all interested firms. Reports received by Navy officials indicate that this program is proving highly successful. 

Small Business Administration field offices also are bringing to the attention of small firms in disaster areas items which the General Services Administration wishes to purchase from firms in these areas. 

Small Business Definition for Procurement 

The Small Business Administration has taken action to modify the standard of 500 employees which has been used for years by the Government procurement agencies as the dividing line between large and small firms. The 500 rule had the advantage of easy administration, and in preparing a new definition the difficulties of administration by procurement agencies that have widely scattered offices were given prime consideration. While the Small Business 

Administration has the power to take arbitrary action in issuing a definition, a definition which has the concurrence and agreement of all procuring agencies of the Government will have a wide and enthusiastic acceptance.

The Small Business Administration has organized an Interagency Task Force to evolve a definition of small business which will be flexible and permit the use of either a lower or higher standard depending on the field of operations. A proposed definition has been published in the Federal Register requesting suggestions, and a redrafting subcommittee is currently placing a new definition in final form.

The essential elements of the new definition will be: 1. 

A small business concern for the purpose of Government procurement is a concern that is (a) not dominant in its field of operation and employs fewer than 500 employees, including the employees of its affiliates, or (b) has been certified as a small business concern by the 

Small Business Administration.

نه 3. 

In the submission of a bid or proposal on a specific Government procurement, a concern may represent that it is a small business concern under part 1(a) of the above definition and in the absence of challenge, such concern will thereby qualify as a small business concern for the purpose of Government procurement. 

A business concern may qualify for a small business certificate as described in part 1(b) of the above definition and as provided in the Act, by applying to the 

Small Business Administration regional office for the region in which the applicant has its principal place of business. When appropriate, a small business certificate will be issued and such concern will thereby qualify as a small business concern for the purpose of Government procurement. 

The foregoing will modify the 500-rule by: 1. 2. 

Excluding those concerns which employ fewer than 500 employees but are (a) dominant or (b) not independent. Such concerns may be excluded either as a result of being challenged or otherwise being denied a small business certificate. All other concerns in this group may self-qualify as small business.

Including some concerns which are (a) not dominant, and (b) independent, in the above 

500-group. The "certifying" process will be used for this purpose. Concerns which are relatively small in their industry may qualify as small business even though they employ more than 500. Any such size determination will be made after giving due consideration to the objectives of the Act.

Eligible concerns may self-certify, thus relieving the 

Small Business Administration of the administrative burden of issuing a "multitude" of certificates. Thus, it will be necessary for a challenge or application for certificate to be processed only in borderline cases.

The new definition will allow participation by concerns actually small in their fields of operation. The "certifying" process may be used to utilize fully the criteria set forth in the Act as a means to qualify concerns in the above 500-group. Thus, the present 

500-employee rule under the new definition will be given flexibility, and the competitive situation in industries more accurately reflected. 

It should result in greater equity without imposing burdensome administrative functions.

CHAPTER V PRODUCTION AND MANAGEMENT ASSISTANCE 

During the last 6-months period, the Small Business Administration provided small firms many types of production and management assistance: 1. 

-- 
-- 
It gave small businesses advice and assistance in obtaining materials and equipment. 

It worked to insure for small business a fair share of the benefits of accelerated tax amortization.

It gave advice and assistance in the formation of small business production pools.

It substantially improved the small business 

It gave technical production assistance to individual small business concerns to help solve their problems.

It made available to small concerns information on Government packing and packaging specifications to assist them in selling their services and products to the Government. 

It assisted individual small business concerns in surplus labor areas in order to help expand employment.

It helped small firms obtain technical information and data on product research and development problems. 83

It granted certificates of competency enabling small firms to obtain Government prime contracts which otherwise would have been denied them, not only helping the small firms but also bringing savings to the 

Government. 

It continued its program of helpful publications for small business, including Management, Technical, and Small Marketers Aids and Management booklets. 

Cosponsored administrative management courses in co-operation with 58 universities and other educational institutions throughout the Nation through a program of educational courses for small business owners and managers.

Materials and Equipment Assistance 

Difficulties of small firms in obtaining a fair share of materials which are in critical supply increased during the past 6 months, and the Small Business Administration received numerous requests for assistance in obtaining aluminum, nickel, steel shapes, iron and steel scrap, gypsum, cement, glass and other materials. Some of the small firms reported that it was virtually impossible to obtain needed materials unless they had historical purchasing patterns with the suppliers, Newly established small firms found it extremely difficult to obtain materials which were in scarce supply and were being allotted to old customers. 

Throughout 1955, continuing efforts were required to assist small business concerns to obtain supplies of aluminum. 

Agency staff members, acting on behalf of individual small enterprises, made numerous contacts with the Office of Defense 

Mobilization, General Services Administration, Business and Defense Services Administration of the Department of Commerce, and industrial suppliers. The Small Business Administration regional and Washington offices handled approximately 230 aluminum cases during the last 6 months of the year, and the Agency's efforts resulted in small firms receiving approximately 1, 376, 000 pounds of aluminum which otherwise would not have been made available to them.

Almost 200, 000 additional tons of primary aluminum is expected to come on the American market this year as a result of expansion of existing facilities and construction of new ones. It is anticipated that this additional supply will, to some extent at least, ease the aluminum situation for small businesses. 

Strikes at both overseas sources and domestic plants seriously affected the supply of refined copper during 1955, and until November total cumulative consumption failed to equal that of 1954. When viewed in the light of the year's increase in industrial activity over that of 1954, the seriousness of the problem facing small concerns is apparent. Had it not been for frequent diversions of copper from stockpile shipments, the condition of some small firms would have become critical. 

However, the anticipated domestic production of refined copper is expected to be increased at mid-year by 7, 000 tons per month from new facilities now under construction. 

Another problem with which the Small Business Administration has been concerned is the shortage in the supply of nickel. Although the Government has diverted to industry a number of shipments of nickel scheduled for the stockpile, the method of distribution has not proved satisfactory for small firms. Many concerns are remelt or secondary-ingot producers, and supplying such firms with primary nickel has not been encouraged on the basis that only specific industries would benefit by the nickel used to upgrade scrap for remelt purposes. The nickel-producing industry has also distributed the metal by allotment according to the historical-use pattern of its customers, with obvious adverse effects on new small business enterprises which lack a historical experience. 

On September 23, 1955, Senator John Sparkman,

Chairman of the Senate Small Business Committee, called attention to the increasing number of complaints from small business concerns which find it impossible to purchase adequate supplies of steel, copper, nickel, aluminum, and other metals. 

Senator Sparkman said, "Small-business men, it seems, are generally dissatisfied with the practice whereby basic producers distribute metals on an 'historical basis'." On October 13, 1955, Administrator Barnes replied to the Senator stating that "for years the large corporations have solicited business from small concerns during periods of low or moderate production, only to ignore them during periods of high productivity in favor of large-tonnage

customers. " Mr. Barnes went on to say that he was not in favor of economic controls in peacetime but "basic industries such as steel, copper, nickel and aluminum might better fill the needs of new enterprises if they were to enter into voluntary programs to issue equitable supplies to new members of each industry, both large and small. "

The production of machine tools during the last 6 months has, in general, been adequate to meet the needs of small firms, although the Small Business Administration has been asked frequently to locate commercial sources of used equipment, or surplus Government-owned equipment. 

The Office of Defense Mobilization asks Small Business 

Administration review of each request from the Department of Defense for approval to lease Government-owned machine tools for nondefense purposes. The purpose of the Agency's review is to see that such leases do not adversely affect the competitive position of small concerns. When it appears that a proposed lease might have this effect, the Agency makes appropriate recom mendations to the Office of Defense Mobilization. 

At the request of the Office of Defense Mobilization, the 

Small Business Administration participated in an interagency committee to establish a program for making Government-owned machine tools available to plants whose machinery was damaged or destroyed in the floods of August 1955. 

Tax Amortization Program 

The Agency performs two principal functions in assisting small firms regarding tax amortization. At the request of the 

Office of Defense Mobilization and the delegate agencies, the Small Business Administration field offices obtain additional information about small business applications, when these lack certain essential data. In other cases, the Agency is asked by a small firm to check on the status of its application and advise it of any adverse developments or other circumstances of which it should be informed. Both operations require continuing close liaison with the Office of Defense 

Mobilization and the delegate agencies.

Although many of the Government's expansion goals have now been filled, the Agency still receives requests from small

firms for assistance in this area. 

At present, there are but 34 open expansion goals, few of which require facilities of the type likely to be built by small concerns.

Production Pools The Small Business Administration gives advice and assistance in the formation of small business production pools, and formally approves their proposed operations in accordance with section 217 of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended. 

This approval is subject to the concurrence of the Attorney General and the Chairman of the Federal Trade Commission. 

Production pools consist primarily of groups of small concerns voluntarily associated together to obtain and perform jointly contracts for the production of articles, equipment, supplies and materials, or for the furnishing of services, needed for the defense effort. The firms have a mutual agreement as to their organization, relationship and procedure.

Interest in the formation of production pools has greatly declined, as evidenced by the fact that no new pools were approved by the Agency during the past 6 months and only two formal requests for approval are pending.

Nineteen of the previously approved pools have been disbanded, and consequently such exemptions from the effect of the antitrust laws which these production pools have enjoyed under the provisions of section 217 of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended, have been withdrawn. This reduces the total number of approved pools to 5, with 63 member companies and approximately 

3, 275 employees. Small Business Administration regional offices report approximately 40 inquiries regarding the formation of production pools during the past 6 months. In addition, the Washington office received a number of direct inquiries. 

Contracts awarded to approved pools now total 227, amounting to approximately $3, 175, 000. The pools also have reported receipt of 51 subcontracts totaling $2,817, 042.

Inventory of Small Plant Facilities The Small Business Administration's facilities inventory is a nationwide plan for registering the productive facilities of small companies. Data on their industrial potential is supplied by small firms on a voluntary basis. 

The registration of small concerns is conducted according to a decentralized plan, with the Agency's regional and branch offices responsible for maintaining the inventory. The field offices work directly with small firms which ask to be placed on the inventory list.

The companies are furnished a questionnaire on which they enter information pertaining to their machinery and equipment, capacity, number of employees, type of products, and other essential manufacturing data. The data are then analyzed by the regional staff to determine the nature of the facility and its proper coding in the Standard Industrial Classification. Uses of the facilities inventory include (1) assistance to the registered plants in obtaining contracts, (2) furnishing of additional bidders to Government agencies, and (3) locating specialized or scarce machine tools and equipment. 

An additional purpose is to have in readiness reliable operating data on small firms and an inventory which can be expanded rapidly if defense conditions warrant.

As of December 31, 1955, there were 29, 753 small companies registered with the Agency. Most of these were manufacturing plants. However, listings of distributive and service firms are increasing, in recognition of the Small Business Administration's broader responsibility to all small business. 

Production Assistance 

Small plant operators frequently are confronted with technical and production problems for which outside counsel is needed. The Small Business Administration provides such assistance through regional office staff members with professional backgrounds in engineering, production and related fields. For example, the staff members answer questions relating to conversion of existing facilities for defense production work, acquisition of new equipment, plant layout, methods engineering, and resumption of operations following a disaster.

If a regional production or industrial specialist finds that a small company's problems are of sufficient scope or complexity, he advises it to retain professional consultants. The 

Agency is careful to avoid entering into competition with the professional field.

Some typical problems referred to the Agency, and the methods of solution, follow: 

1. A Missouri manufacturer of wood products asked the

Small Business Administration how he could use waste to make salable by-products. As a result of Agency suggestions and guidance, he now has six kilns making charcoal from waste wood, and is selling hickory sawdust to flavor charcoal used for barbecuing meat. This has greatly increased his net income, and provides a cash reserve which allows him to hold his higher grade lumber for sale as air-dried flooring lumber. Formerly, it was necessary for him to sell green lumber at a lower price in order to maintain working capital. 

2. A small concern in Texas obtained a Government contract, and almost overnight found its vehicle repair and maintenance business tripled. Operating efficiency of the company was hampered by inadequate production and management methods. 

A Small Business Administration representative suggested a

dispatching system, changed invoicing methods, and a clearer organizational pattern. The company's management, pleased with the improved efficiency, has now hired a private consulting firm to make more detailed studies of its operations. 

3. While making a plant survey in Colorado, an 

Agency representative learned that the company needed a special electrical heating element. By means of the Small Business 

Administration's facilities inventory, the company was directed to another Colorado concern which could design and manufacture the required heating element. 

4. A small plant in the State of Washington was advised by the Agency's Seattle Regional Office to eliminate the hand assembly mandrel on its product and substitute another type. The suggested change will result in an estimated yearly saving to the company of $7, 200. 

5. The Boston Regional Office has assisted a New 

England company in the selection of a plant location in the development of methods, equipment, plant layout and cost studies, and in business and production management. The company, which is expanding, will locate its new plant to repackage nonfat milk solids in a surplus labor area of Massachusetts. One official of the company states that Small Business Administration assistance and advice on this project have been invaluable. 

Specifications Assistance 

A continuing problem of small concerns is difficulty in obtaining copies of Federal and military specifications. Many small firms receive bid sets without the appropriate specifications, or with the basic specification alone, while all supplementary specifications are omitted. Frequently, when a small firm writes to the purchasing agency for the needed specifications, it finds that the heavy demand for them has exhausted the supply. 

The Small Business Administration maintains at its Washington and field offices a 4-volume reference set of the packaging and packing specifications most often required by small concerns in selling their products and services to the Government. 

These specifications are periodically reviewed by the Agency to eliminate canceled or superseded specifications, and to insure the inclusion of new or revised specifications and amendments to existing ones.

As an additional convenience to small concerns, Small Business Administration field offices maintain files of Government specifications compiled from expired bid sets, and make these limited stocks available to small firms in need of them. 

The Small Business Administration also makes available to small firms the "U. S. Government Specifications 

Directory, "a guide to more than 500 places throughout the country where small business owners may locate quickly Government specifications and indexes needed to prepare bids on Government purchases. The directory discusses the purchasing specifications used by the Government; tells how businessmen may obtain copies of bid specifications and specifications indexes for continuing use; and provides pointers on using them. 

In the period July 1 through December 31, 1955, the

Small Business Administration received from the General 

Services Administration 132 proposed new or interim Federal specifications. These were reviewed by the Agency to make certain their provisions would not be restrictive to small concerns. 

The Agency's analyses have resulted in a number of recommendations to the General Services Administration for deletion of possible restrictive or discriminatory provisions. 

The Small Business Administration also seeks revisions of existing specifications to make it possible for small manufacturers to have their products considered in Government purchasing. 

Toward this end, negotiations have been held during the past 6 months with the Quartermaster Corps, Air Force, Navy, Veterans

Administration, Department of Justice and Post Office Department. 

Surplus Labor Areas 

Though the over-all problem of surplus labor areas has improved, the Small Business Administration continues to receive occasional requests for assistance. Many of the requests are concerned chiefly with area development problems, such as locating new industries for communities, and therefore are referred to the Office of Area Development, Department of Commerce.

Small Business Administration assistance to surplus labor areas is confined to help for individual small business

concerns in the areas.

The Agency's assistance is of three types: 

Help in obtaining prime contracts and subcontracts, technical production, and financial assistance. 

The usual procedure here is for Agency representatives to meet with community leaders and groups of small business executives. The problems are discussed, the Small Business Administration's programs of technical, procurement, new products, managerial and financial assistance are outlined, and individual meetings are arranged with small business concerns. At these meetings, Agency staff members explore with the firms possible ways to expand or diversify their production and thus absorb the area's surplus labor.

In one instance, exploratory meetings held in Huntington, West Virginia, in December 1954 have now resulted in the formation of a defense production pool by small business concerns to permit them to bid on larger contracts than the individual concerns could handle successfully.

Products Assistance Program The Small Business Administration's Products Assistance 

Program is designed to assist small concerns gain access to needed product development information available from Government and Since the start of the program in July 1954, requests industry. for this type of assistance have increased steadily. 

The purposes of the program are: 1. 2. 

To assist small concerns gain access to data on research and on product, process and market development needed in solving day-to-day operating problems, and in planning production and marketing programs. 

To provide small firms with a clearing house for information on available products and processes which can be used to maintain, diversify, or expand their operations. 

Through this program, the Small Business Administration has provided technical information and data for inquiring small firms on a vast range of subjects. Typical of the problems submitted

for solutions are: How to eliminate a costly process; where to find a substitute; how to use or where to sell a by-product or waste; what is the income of a given area for market purposes; what are the Government regulations concerning specific products in interstate commerce and what industrial uses have been developed for agricultural products. Since the Small Business 

Administration does not itself engage in research and development or conduct market surveys, requests for assistance with problems of this nature are submitted to the best sources in industry and Government for recommendations. Industrial organizations, trade associations, Government agencies and educational institutions all contribute to solving the problems. The information thus obtained is forwarded to the small concern with full credit to the informational sources.

Since the start of the Products Assistance Program in July 1954, more than 6, 500 requests for information and data to solve problems for small concerns have been processed. 

Another part of the program is designed to provide small concerns information on new and improved products and processes available for manufacture or marketing. The Agency's Products 

List Circular, issued monthly, lists abstracts of inventions which have been made available for production and commercial distribution by inventors, patent attorneys, private firms, Government agencies, and research and product development organizations. 

In the last 6 months of 1955, 445 inventions were listed resulting in an additional 1, 200 inquiries. More than 5, 000 individuals and small concerns asked to receive the circular regularly. 

The Products Assistance Program has been particularly helpful to small concerns which are unable to maintain continuous research activities essential to their successful operation, or whose owners cannot devote the time needed to search out solutions to product development problems. Many small concerns also have been helped to obtain information on the availability of new and improved products and processes.

The following examples illustrate the nature of problems for which the Agency provided recommended solutions: 1. 

Species and sources of supply of hardwood best suited for dowel pins in manufacture of furniture.

2. 

Marketing of a new tumbling device used to burnish and deburr metal parts. 3. 

Silvering mirrors by electrolysis. 

Percentage of lead in the manufacture of lacquer which would be considered toxic. 5. 6. 

Method of reclaiming silver from used photographic solutions and films; also commercial outlets for the reclaimed silver. 

Method of exploding sand into tiny bubbles to produce lightweight aggregate for concrete suitable for manufacture of concrete blocks and similar items. 7. 

Method of bleaching shellac. 8.

Sources producing equipment for packaging individual servings of sauces, jellies and table condiments. 9. 

Method of molding plastic bottles. 10. 

Sales outlets and commercial uses for baled wheat and oat straw. 11.

Sources of supply of unfinished driftwood for artistic purposes. 12.

New uses and commercial outlets for coke oven by-product known as light oil of coal tar residue. 13.

Manufacture of clothing protective against radioactive materials.

Certificates of Competency The Certificate of Competency Program of the Small 

Business Administration benefits small companies by helping them

obtain Government prime contracts which otherwise would be denied to them. A certificate of competency is a statement from the Administrator of the Agency addressed to the contracting officer of a Government procurement agency certifying that the small company named therein is qualified to perform a specific contract on which it has submitted a bid. 

If a small company or group of small companies is the low bidder, or within negotiation range, on a proposed Government contract, but is being rejected because of a question of credit or capacity, or both, the company or group may apply to the Small Business Administration for a certificate. The Agency then makes a comprehensive field survey of the firm's facilities and an evaluation of its manufacturing experience, technical ability and financial capabilities. If the Agency finds that the bidder actually is qualified to perform the contract, it issues a certificate of competency attesting to this. The certificate must be accepted as conclusive by the procurement officer as far as technical and financial requirements of the contract are concerned.

Small Business Administration regional offices now have authority to take final action on applications for certificates when they agree with the adverse finding of the procurement agency. 

This decentralized procedure results in more rapid disposition of some cases. All other applications are examined by the regional offices and sent, with the field recommendations, to the national office for final review and decision.

After a contract is awarded as the result of issuance of a certificate of competency, its progress is watched closely by a 

Small Business Administration representative. If the contractor experiences production or other difficulties, the Agency offers assistance with the problems.

The following summary shows the status of the 

Certificate of Competency Program as of December 31, 1955:

In addition, the Small Business Administration is servicing three certified cases which it assumed from its predecessor, the Small Defense Plants Administration. Work on these contracts is still underway. 

Contract awards resulting from the issuance of 

Small Business Administration certificates of competency have gone to a wide variety of small enterprises; for example, to firms producing wearing apparel, chemicals, electrical and electronic equipment, surgical accessories, and hand tools.

The largest number of certificate applications arise from purchase actions of the Air Force depots and Army procurement agencies, principally the Signal Corps, Quartermaster Corps, and Ordnance Corps. Applications based on 

Navy purchases are less numerous. The table on page 97 shows the number of applications received, the purchasing agency involved, and the certificates issued. 

Managerial Assistance 

Managerial assistance for small firms is provided by the Small Business Administration through management publications, management education, and management counseling. 

Management Publications 

Through its management publications, the Small Business Administration has pointed the way to better management to many thousands of small business owners throughout the United States. 

The 4 series of management publications issued by the Agency now cover 131 different subjects. (For a complete listing of these publications see Appendix J, page 127.)

Management Aids for Small Manufacturers 

To date, 68 of these 4-page, free leaflets have been issued. More recent aids include "Using Your Banker's Advisory 

Services," "Developing Sound Production Standards," "How the

Securities Act of 1933 Affects Small Business," and "Appeal Procedure for Income Tax Cases." Total distribution of the 

Management Aids is now 1, 554, 875 copies, of which 144, 525 copies were distributed on request during the past 6 months.

Small Marketers Aids 

This series is designed to provide helpful information to smaller retail, wholesale and service enterprises. Typical of the leaflets in the series are: "Records Management in Small 

Stores," "Effective Advertising for Small Retailers," and "Basic

Stock Control for Small Stores. " Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers 

Today technological changes affect many production aspects of the smallest manufacturing plant or shop. These changes involve production processes, materials, machinery and equipment, and maintenance. Hence, the Technical Aids leaflets are issued to supply useful and needed information on a variety of manufacturing problems. 

To date, 40 Technical Aids have been published. More recent Aids include: "Reinforced Plastics," "Preventive Mainte nance for the Small Plant," and "Reducing Costs with Jigs, Fixtures, and Gages." The number of copies of Technical Aids distributed now totals 479,645, of which 6, 200 were distributed during the past 6 months.

Small Business Management Series 

When a management subject cannot be adequately covered in a short leaflet, the Small Business Administration has a leading authority in the field prepare a manuscript on the subject. The manuscript, purchased under contract, is published as one of the Agency's Small Business Management Series booklets. 

The booklets are sold at nominal prices by the Government Printing Office, and widespread acceptance of them is indicated by sale of more than 350, 000 copies of the 17 booklets issued thus far. Among the best sellers are "Cost Accounting for Small 

Manufacturers," with sales of 35, 400; "Cutting Office Costs in

Small Plants," with sales of 34, 624; "Human Relations in Small 

Industry," with sales of 30, 930; and "A Handbook of Small 

Business Finance, " with sales of 26, 174.

Joint Small Business Administration-Commerce Publications 

The joint management publications program of the 

Small Business Administration and the Department of Commerce was terminated for lack of funds at the end of fiscal year 1955. 

During its operation, five series of publications of interest to small business were developed. Among them were: (1) Catalogs of more than 100, 000 technical reports; (2) catalogs of more than 

7,000 selected industrial and commercial films available to business; and (3) catalogs of Government-owned inventions. All of these publications are available from the Office of Technical Services, Department of Commerce at nominal prices. 

Other joint series were (4) Operating Ratio Sheets and Financial Requirement Work Sheets relating to specific retail and service trades; and (5) Basic Information Bulletins. These leaflets may still be obtained without charge from the Washington or field offices of the Small Business Administration or the Department of 

Commerce. 

Management Education 

In co-operation with universities and other educational groups throughout the Nation, the Small Business Administration is cosponsoring short courses in administrative management for small business owners and managers. 

These courses are designed to help the small business operator improve his over-all knowledge of business administration, to learn more about planning, organizing, control, and other phases of management. Statistics compiled by Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., show that 9 times out of 10 business failures "can be traced directly to lack of experience or lack of aptitude for running a business." In elaborating on this fact Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., adds that the major portion of failures results from lack of managerial experience, unbalanced experience (inexperience in one or more of the principal phases of running a business), and incompetence.

By co-operating with educational institutions, as provided in the Small Business Act, the Agency is encouraging wide dissemination of information on good management practices.

The educational institutions assume educational and financial responsibility for all the courses, and the Small Business Administration lends assistance in the role of cosponsor, often with the co-operation of local organizations such as the clearing house association (banks); chamber of commerce or junior chamber of commerce; local or State associations of manufacturers, retailers, or wholesalers; and individual trade associations. Costs are defrayed by tuition fees paid by the businessmen who take the courses. 

Two pilot

During the school year 1954-55, there were 54 of these courses offered by 40 educational institutions. courses were offered prior to this in the spring of 1954, making a total of 56 courses completed by September 1955. 

Since September 1955, 26 courses have been completed, 23 are now in operation, and 31 others are scheduled for future operation during the 1955-56 school year. These 80 courses have been or are being given by 58 educational institutions. Ten institutions have conducted or are conducting two or more courses. 

Evaluations from almost 750 businessmen who have attended these courses have been analyzed and their suggestions are being used by educational institutions in conducting the new courses. Nearly all of the businessmen made favorable comments. 

Of those who rated the, courses as a whole, 86 per cent stated they were good to excellent, while 13. 5 per cent rated them fair. Only 

3 persons rated them as poor. More than 95 per cent of the students asked said that the problems discussed were important or vital to operation of their businesses.

Footnotes to Balance Sheet Lending Operations 

1/ Covers only regular lending operation of Small Business Administration, under the provisions of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended. 

Operations with respect to liquidation of RFC Disaster Loans, transferred under the provisions of Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1954, are described in separate exhibits. 

2/ Net of reserve for retroactive adjustment of interest from 6 to 3 per cent for those disaster loans approved temporarily under SBA business loan authority and to be converted to 3 per cent rate when legislative limitation is raised by the Congress. 

3/ Liabilities exclude $33,464,600.71 undisbursed loan commitments, on a closed loan basis, as of December 31, 1955. As exhibited in the acconpanying "Status of Revolving Fund" statement, the undisbursed loan commitments, on a checks-issued basis, aggregate $29,779,425.97. The difference, $3,685,174.74, represents checks issued by the Treasury but on December 31, 1955 not yet delivered to payees, exclusive of banks' shares thereof ($8,950.00) pertaining to disbursements on 

Immediate Participation Loans Serviced by SBA.

1/ Covers only regular lending operation of Small Business Administration. 

Excludes $900,406.44, the net cost for the first half of Fiscal Year 

1956 of other programs for which the Small Business Administration is responsible, exclusive of the operations with respect to liquidation of RFC Disaster Loans, transferred under the provisions of Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1954, which are described in separate exhibits. 

2/ Net of provision for retroactive adjustment of interest from 6 to 3 per cent for those disaster loans approved temporarily under SBA business loan authority and to be converted to 3 per cent rate when legislative limitation is raised by the Congress. 3/ This provision has been made in order to accumulate reserves for possible losses, based on Government lending agency experience factors developed over a period of years, applicable to loan disbursements under the various programs.
